My anxiety seems to be mostly tied to my recent work promotion and all the new responsibilities that came with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or difficulty sleeping that accompany your feelings of anxiety and worry?\n\nPatient: I don't really have tremors or sweating, but my sleep is terrible—I'm often awake until 2 a.m. replaying work emails and wake up feeling completely unrefreshed. I also feel constantly wired and physically tense, like I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your excessive worry is focused primarily on one specific aspect of your life, such as work, or if it's more generalized and encompasses multiple areas, like work, finances, relationships, and daily life?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just about work, though that is a huge trigger for me. Once the worry starts, it quickly spirals into everything else, like my student loans, my parents' health, and this constant fear that I'm going to lose my apartment or disappoint my family. It feels like my mind is always finding some new disaster to focus on.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many different areas of your life you regularly worry about, and are there any specific themes that your worries tend to revolve around most frequently?\n\nPatient: I'd say I worry about almost every area of my life, and it feels completely overwhelming. The main themes are work, like messing up automation scripts, failing the people I mentor, or getting fired, but I also constantly stress about my parents' health, disappointing my family, and financial things like student loans and losing my apartment. It just feels like there's always some disaster waiting to happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you feel restless or on edge, and does it seem to be triggered by specific situations or is it a more constant feeling for you?\n\nPatient: It's a constant feeling for me, and I feel wired and on edge most of the time rather than just in specific situations. It honestly feels like I've had way too much coffee even when I haven't, to the point where I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot. I can't seem to just sit and breathe without feeling like something is about to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, like your dance nights and hikes, or has your anxiety primarily affected your ability to participate in them due to worry rather than a loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I definitely haven't lost interest in them, and I actually really miss the balance they used to give me. I've just been skipping them because I feel completely exhausted, anxious, and overwhelmed by trying to keep up with my heavy work queue and other commitments.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or beliefs about yourself that you worry about, such as being a failure or not meeting expectations, or if your worries are more focused on external events and circumstances?"
  }

It has gotten to the point where I feel completely overwhelmed, so a friend suggested I finally come in to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any panic attacks, where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, or if your worrying is more of a constant, ongoing state?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience those sudden, peak-and-fade panic attacks; it's definitely more of a constant, ongoing state of worry. My mind is always running worst-case scenarios like a YouTube autoplay I can't pause, and I just feel wired and on edge almost all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations, places, or activities because of your worries or fears?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I've started canceling plans with my friends and skipping my usual dance nights and hikes. I just feel too anxious, exhausted, and completely drained by the constant worrying to keep up with them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your current state of anxiety and worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any traumatic events in my past that I can think of. My anxiety seems to be mostly tied to my recent work promotion and all the new responsibilities that came with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or difficulty sleeping that accompany your feelings of anxiety and worry?\n\nPatient: I don't really have tremors or sweating, but my sleep is terrible—I'm often awake until 2 a.m. replaying work emails and wake up feeling completely unrefreshed. I also feel constantly wired and physically tense, like I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your excessive worry is focused primarily on one specific aspect of your life, such as work, or if it's more generalized and encompasses multiple areas, like work, finances, relationships, and daily life?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just about work, though that is a huge trigger for me. It honestly feels like I've had way too much coffee even when I haven't, to the point where I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot. I can't seem to just sit and breathe without feeling like something is about to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, like your dance nights and hikes, or has your anxiety primarily affected your ability to participate in them due to worry rather than a loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I definitely haven't lost interest in them, and I actually really miss the balance they used to give me. I've just been skipping them because I feel completely exhausted, anxious, and overwhelmed by trying to keep up with my heavy work queue and other commitments.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or beliefs about yourself that you worry about, such as being a failure or not meeting expectations, or if your worries are more focused on external events and circumstances?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I worry deeply about failing and not meeting expectations, like making a mistake on a script or saying something stupid while mentoring and disappointing everyone. At the same time, my mind also gets completely overwhelmed by external things I can't control, like my student loans and my parents' health.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any periods in the past six months where you felt completely free from these worries and anxiety for at least a couple of days, or has it been a persistent issue?\n\nPatient: No, it has been completely persistent without any breaks at all. For the entire nine months since my promotion, there hasn't been a single day where the worry and tension went away, not even on weekends when I'm trying to relax. It has just been a constant weight."

It just feels like there's always some disaster waiting to happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you feel restless or on edge, and does it seem to be triggered by specific situations or is it a more constant feeling for you?\n\nPatient: It's a constant feeling for me, and I feel wired and on edge most of the time rather than just in specific situations. It honestly feels like I've had way too much coffee even when I haven't, to the point where I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot. I can't seem to just sit and breathe without feeling like something is about to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, like your dance nights and hikes, or has your anxiety primarily affected your ability to participate in them due to worry rather than a loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I definitely haven't lost interest in them, and I actually really miss the balance they used to give me. I've just been skipping them because I feel completely exhausted, anxious, and overwhelmed by trying to keep up with my heavy work queue and other commitments.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or beliefs about yourself that you worry about, such as being a failure or not meeting expectations, or if your worries are more focused on external events and circumstances?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I worry deeply about failing and not meeting expectations, like making a mistake on a script or saying something stupid while mentoring and disappointing everyone. At the same time, my mind also gets completely overwhelmed by external things I can't control, like my student loans and my parents' health.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any periods in the past six months where you felt completely free from these worries and anxiety for at least a couple of days, or has it been a persistent issue?\n\nPatient: No, it has been completely persistent without any breaks at all. For the entire nine months since my promotion, there hasn't been a single day where the worry and tension went away, not even on weekends when I'm trying to relax. It has just been a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your worries span multiple areas of your life, including work, finances, relationships, and daily life. Can you clarify if there's a predominant theme or area that your worries most frequently return to, or do they seem to shift fairly evenly across these different domains?\n\nPatient: Work is definitely the most frequent starting point or trigger, but the worries don't stay there. Once they start, they quickly cascade and shift to other things like my student loans, my parents' health, and disappointing my family. It really feels like a chain reaction where the focus is constantly moving rather than staying on just one thing."

At the same time, my mind also gets completely overwhelmed by external things I can't control, like my student loans and my parents' health.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any periods in the past six months where you felt completely free from these worries and anxiety for at least a couple of days, or has it been a persistent issue?\n\nPatient: No, it has been completely persistent without any breaks at all. For the entire nine months since my promotion, there hasn't been a single day where the worry and tension went away, not even on weekends when I'm trying to relax. It has just been a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your worries span multiple areas of your life, including work, finances, relationships, and daily life. Can you clarify if there's a predominant theme or area that your worries most frequently return to, or do they seem to shift fairly evenly across these different domains?\n\nPatient: Work is definitely the most frequent starting point or trigger, but the worries don't stay there. Once they start, they quickly cascade and shift to other things like my student loans, my parents' health, and disappointing my family. It really feels like a chain reaction where the focus is constantly moving rather than staying on just one thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling physically tense, like you're always clenching your shoulders and tapping your foot. How often would you say you experience this muscle tension, is it present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is pretty much constant and present throughout most of the day. I feel so wired and on edge, almost like I've had way too much coffee, and I find myself clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot from morning until night. It makes it incredibly difficult to just sit, breathe, and actually relax."

At the same time, my mind also gets completely overwhelmed by external things I can't control, like my student loans and my parents' health.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any periods in the past six months where you felt completely free from these worries and anxiety for at least a couple of days, or has it been a persistent issue?\n\nPatient: No, it has been completely persistent without any breaks at all. It has just been a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your worries span multiple areas of your life, including work, finances, relationships, and daily life. Can you clarify if there's a predominant theme or area that your worries most frequently return to, or do they seem to shift fairly evenly across these different domains?\n\nPatient: Work is definitely the most frequent starting point or trigger, but the worries don't stay there. Once they start, they quickly cascade and shift to other things like my student loans, my parents' health, and disappointing my family. It really feels like a chain reaction where the focus is constantly moving rather than staying on just one thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling physically tense, like you're always clenching your shoulders and tapping your foot. How often would you say you experience this muscle tension, is it present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is pretty much constant and present throughout most of the day. I feel so wired and on edge, almost like I've had way too much coffee, and I find myself clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot from morning until night. It makes it incredibly difficult to just sit, breathe, and actually relax.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your excessive worry is typically accompanied by feelings of fear, or is it more about the anticipation of potential problems or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: It is definitely more about anticipating potential problems and making mistakes rather than a sudden feeling of fear. My mind is constantly running worst-case scenarios, like worrying that I'll mess up a coding script for my big client, say something stupid while mentoring, or cause some disaster that gets me fired. It just feels like I'm always trying to prevent a catastrophe before it happens."

The main themes are work, like messing up automation scripts, failing the people I mentor, or getting fired, but I also constantly stress about my parents' health, disappointing my family, and financial things like student loans and losing my apartment. It just feels like there's always some disaster waiting to happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you feel restless or on edge, and does it seem to be triggered by specific situations or is it a more constant feeling for you?\n\nPatient: It's a constant feeling for me, and I feel wired and on edge most of the time rather than just in specific situations. It honestly feels like I've had way too much coffee even when I haven't, to the point where I'm always clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot. I can't seem to just sit and breathe without feeling like something is about to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, like your dance nights and hikes, or has your anxiety primarily affected your ability to participate in them due to worry rather than a loss of interest?\n\nPatient: I definitely haven't lost interest in them, and I actually really miss the balance they used to give me. I've just been skipping them because I feel completely exhausted, anxious, and overwhelmed by trying to keep up with my heavy work queue and other commitments.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or beliefs about yourself that you worry about, such as being a failure or not meeting expectations, or if your worries are more focused on external events and circumstances?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I worry deeply about failing and not meeting expectations, like making a mistake on a script or saying something stupid while mentoring and disappointing everyone. At the same time, my mind also gets completely overwhelmed by external things I can't control, like my student loans and my parents' health.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any periods in the past six months where you felt completely free from these worries and anxiety for at least a couple of days, or has it been a persistent issue?\n\nPatient: No, it has been completely persistent without any breaks at all. For the entire nine months since my promotion, there hasn't been a single day where the worry and tension went away, not even on weekends when I'm trying to relax. It has just been a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your worries span multiple areas of your life, including work, finances, relationships, and daily life. Can you clarify if there's a predominant theme or area that your worries most frequently return to, or do they seem to shift fairly evenly across these different domains?\n\nPatient: Work is definitely the most frequent starting point or trigger, but the worries don't stay there. Once they start, they quickly cascade and shift to other things like my student loans, my parents' health, and disappointing my family. It really feels like a chain reaction where the focus is constantly moving rather than staying on just one thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling physically tense, like you're always clenching your shoulders and tapping your foot. How often would you say you experience this muscle tension, is it present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is pretty much constant and present throughout most of the day. I feel so wired and on edge, almost like I've had way too much coffee, and I find myself clenching my shoulders and tapping my foot from morning until night. It makes it incredibly difficult to just sit, breathe, and actually relax.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your excessive worry is typically accompanied by feelings of fear, or is it more about the anticipation of potential problems or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: It is definitely more about anticipating potential problems and making mistakes rather than a sudden feeling of fear. My mind is constantly running worst-case scenarios, like worrying that I'll mess up a coding script for my big client, say something stupid while mentoring, or cause some disaster that gets me fired. It just feels like I'm always trying to prevent a catastrophe before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a recent situation where you felt your worry or anxiety was disproportionate to the actual threat or danger, and how you responded to it?\n\nPatient: Just last week, a minor task like writing an automation script triggered this massive spiral where I convinced myself I'd mess it up, get fired, lose my apartment, and disappoint my family. I responded by staying up all night triple-checking the configuration and running through hypothetical failures, which left me completely exhausted. I even ended up canceling my dance night just to keep obsessing over it, even though the script was actually fine."

Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
